WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Fuel, housing and the bumpy road ahead
Higher fuel costs, a weaker exchange rate, and a softer
housing market have prompted a refresh of our near-term
forecasts. Inflation looks set to finish 2026 above 4%, while
growth could lose a little momentum into year-end. Even so,

we continue to expect the RBNZ to pause in October before
lifting the OCR to 3% in December.
New Zealand’s recovery is still moving forward, but the road
has become rougher. Since our last forecast update in August,
global oil and refined-fuel prices have climbed sharply, the
New Zealand dollar has fallen, and longer-term interest rates
have moved higher. At home, the housing market has remained
weaker than we anticipated. None of those developments,

on their own, are enough to derail the recovery. Together,
however, they point to a tougher finish to 2026 for households
and businesses. We have therefore revisited our near-term
inflation, housing and growth forecasts. The broad story
remains one of continued economic expansion and gradually
improving labour demand, but with a more pronounced
inflation spike and slightly less momentum in consumer
spending. Our OCR call is unchanged: a pause at the October

review, followed by a 25 basis point increase to 3% at the
December Monetary Policy Statement.
A temporary inflation reprieve
We continue to think that consumer prices rose 0.7% in the
September quarter. That would lower annual inflation from
4.1% in June to 3.7% in September, broadly consistent with
the monthly price updates published so far. The final monthly
release for the quarter is due on 16 October and will help firm up
that estimate. But any easing in the annual rate is likely to prove

short-lived. We now expect prices to rise 1.1% in the December
quarter, lifting annual inflation to a three-year high of 4.2% by
year-end. That is half a percentage point above our previous
projection and above the RBNZ’s September forecast of 3.9%.
The reason is easy to see at the petrol pump. Higher global
fuel prices have been amplified by a more than 4% fall in
the New Zealand dollar since the start of September. Local
petrol prices have risen by around 50 cents a litre over the

past month, with the nationwide average price of 91 unleaded
reaching a record $3.55 a litre this week. RBNZ Governor
Breman acknowledged a couple of weeks ago that higher
oil prices have tilted the risks around the near-term inflation
outlook to the upside. Importantly, however, the RBNZ remains
focused on the medium-term inflation outlook rather than
trying to offset every movement in volatile fuel prices.
For now, we have left our quarterly inflation forecasts for 2027

unchanged. Even so, the higher starting point at the end of this
year means annual inflation is likely to remain firm through much
of 2027. Our updated profile has inflation at 3.7% in March
2027, 2.6% in June, 2.5% in September and 1.7% in December.
That is broadly similar to the RBNZ’s path through the first three
quarters, although our forecast falls more quickly late in the
year. We will take a fuller look at the 2027 inflation and growth
outlook next week alongside the monthly Market Outlook.

Housing fails to find its feet
The housing market has also delivered a disappointment. We
had expected prices to stabilise through the second half of
2026, but sales volumes have continued to weaken, and the
median time required to sell a property has lengthened to 48
days. Average prices have kept edging lower, despite firmer
conditions in parts of the South Island with greater exposure
to the strong export economy. The latest indicators do not
suggest that a decisive turn is close. We have therefore revised

our forecast for national house price growth in 2026 from a
0.6% rise to a 1.4% decline. That incorporates quarterly falls
of 0.5% in September and 0.8% in December.
The headwinds are familiar: the labour market remains soft,
mortgage rates are higher and employment growth is still
moderate. Those forces are likely to keep the market subdued
for some time, with risks tilted towards another weak year
in 2027. Changes to the taxation of capital gains on investor

housing following the 2026 General Election may add another
potential drag.
Kelly Eckhold
Chief Economist NZ

### Page 7

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
7
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Regional outcomes will continue to differ. Tourism- and
agriculture-focused areas should benefit from stronger income
and employment growth, supported by robust commodity export
prices. We have lifted our 2026/27 Fonterra Milk Price forecast to
$9.75/kg and set an opening forecast of $9.50/kg for 2027/28. In

contrast, homeowners in the major North Island cities may have to
wait longer for the wealth boost that comes with rising property
values. Data released this week shows that household wealth
continues to be dragged down by declining housing values.
A little less momentum
The weaker housing backdrop matters for the wider economy.
When petrol absorbs more of the household budget and home
values are drifting lower, consumers tend to become more
cautious. That combination is likely to crimp discretionary

spending in the run-up to Christmas and could take some
steam out of the construction recovery. That’s even though our
own card spending data for September  showed resilience in
consumer spending. Recent business and consumer confidence
surveys have nevertheless remained resilient despite the rise
in energy prices and long-term interest rates. Our short-term
growth forecasts therefore remain broadly intact, but we have
trimmed our December quarter consumer spending projection

and now expect GDP growth of 2.5% over 2026. That is still
a solid result, although the balance of risks has shifted to
the downside.
There are several moving parts to watch. Sustained high fuel
prices would squeeze household purchasing power more than
we have allowed for. The sharp lift in long-term interest rates
may also weigh on activity through 2027, even if shorter-
term borrowing costs evolve as expected. The election result
introduces two-sided risks that are difficult to quantify before

the shape and timing of policy changes are known. Against that,
strong agricultural incomes and signs of improving employment
growth should provide a buffer. We have not changed our 2026
labour market forecasts. Hiring appears to be strengthening
gradually, but it will take time before that improvement
translates into a noticeable fall in the unemployment rate.
The RBNZ should still pause before Christmas
Despite the higher near-term inflation track, we are not

changing our OCR forecast. Since the July Monetary Policy
Review, our view has been that the RBNZ would slow the pace
of increases as the OCR approached the Bank’s estimate of a
neutral 3%. The September meeting clearly signalled a pause
in October and one further rise in December. We continue to
regard that as the most likely path. The latest developments
raise headline inflation but, so far, there is little evidence that
inflation expectations are moving higher or that the fuel shock

is generating broader, persistent price pressure. At the same
time, growth risks warrant some caution.
A pause in October would give the Monetary Policy Committee
time to assess the cumulative effect of the increases already
delivered and to refine its strategy for 2027. Financial
conditions have also tightened from an unexpected source:
longer-term interest rates have risen markedly over the past
six weeks. That increase has at least offset the easing impulse

from the lower exchange rate, even though the weaker
currency is adding to imported inflation in the near term. We
still think the RBNZ is likely to have more work to do in 2027,
but there is plenty of data to come before that judgement
needs to be made.
For now, the best description of the outlook is resilient but
less comfortable. Export incomes remain strong, activity is
expanding and the labour market is beginning to improve. But
households face an awkward combination of record petrol

prices, soft home values and higher borrowing costs. That
mix points to a bumpy end to the year: inflation will be hotter,
spending growth a little cooler and the recovery more uneven
across regions. The RBNZ can afford to wait in October, but if
the economy develops broadly as we expect, a further hike to
3% in December will still be needed.

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: getting the balance right
By Ryan Wells, Illiana Jain & Elliot Clarke
10:46 October 16 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
Australia’s key data release this week was the September Labour Force Survey. Employment surprised
to the downside, -39.3k. However, a 0.2ppt decline in labour force participation from August’s near-
historic high largely offset. The unemployment rate therefore held at 4.7% after August was revised up.

Residual seasonality may be in the mix. Regardless, the take home point from the update is that the

### Page 2

labour market is continuing to ease as employment growth runs below working-age population growth.
We expect the unemployment rate to grind higher into 2027 as tighter financial conditions and cost-of-
living pressures restrict activity growth to a below-trend pace. Although, our forecast peak is near at
4.8%, due by Q2 2027.
This update on the labour market complicates the communication challenge for the RBA. The
September minutes were firmly on the hawkish side, with the discussion focussing on materialising

upside risks to inflation. But, with the unemployment rate now through the RBA’s NAIRU assumption of
4.6%, Governor Bullock’s “a little tight” descriptor for the labour market is becoming harder to defend.
Still, with a clear focus on inflation risks – which also led us to revise up our near-term CPI forecasts in
the October Market Outlook – we still expect a 25bp rate hike at the November meeting.
Over in the US, the headline CPI rose 0.6% as expected in September, taking the annual rate up to

3.7%yr. Energy prices were a key contributor (5.2%, 20.4%yr), while core inflation was a touch above
consensus in the month at 0.3% (2.6%yr). Within the core components, core goods prices gained 0.3%
after being broadly unchanged over the past 6 months (0.7%yr). Core services meanwhile rose 0.4%
(3.1%yr) on a 0.4% increase in shelter (3.2%yr), a 0.6% gain for transport services and a 0.3%
increase in medical care services (2.7%yr and 2.5%yr respectively). Further, the subsequent release of

the PPI pointed to continued upstream price pressures, with the headline and core measures up
6.3%yr and 5.0%yr respectively. The latest Beige Book provided colour on these developments, firms
across multiple districts citing tariffs, insurance and commodity prices as ongoing sources of cost
pressure. Guiding on the risks, many respondents expect further price increases as contracts/hedges
roll off.
Given their telegraphed concerns over inflation, there is enough signal in these reports to warrant a

follow-up hike at the October FOMC meeting. Though it seems Committee members believe they are
near the end of the tightening cycle and so have time on their side. This week, Daly and Bowman both
showed patience in assessing the trend, with no clear bias towards an October or December decision.
Williams stated he believes one more hike would prove sufficient in this cycle given known information.
And Waller also showed confidence, highlighting that elevated long-term yields are doing some of the

FOMC’s work, restricting demand in interest rate sensitive sectors. We hold a similar view, anticipating
that one more hike by year end and a little time will bring inflation near the 2.0%yr medium-term target.
However, as discussed in this month’s Market Outlook, capacity pressures are likely to see inflation
consistently above target, rather than below, in coming years – particularly if, as we suspect, capacity
in the housing sector remains tight. This will mean the FOMC have to remain modestly restrictive over

the forecast horizon and will also bias up long-term yields.
In Asia, there was one release that stood out, China’s trade account. At USD133.5bn, the September
result was a whisker inside January 2025’s record high of USD137.9bn. More impressively, throughout
2025 and 2026 year-to-date the surplus has now averaged USD100.9bn, almost 3 times the average of
2019, prior to the pandemic. The geographic breadth of export growth is as impressive as the headline

annual gain of 36.3%yr, even more so given the ongoing strength in Chinese investment in
neighbouring nations’ production chains. In time, both trade and income receipts from offshore
investment should buoy the renminbi, although for now continued outbound foreign investment should
moderate the pace of gains against other nations in the region, and likely versus the US dollar.

### Page 3

Related articles
Another key trend assessed in October’s Market Outlook is the evolution of producer prices across
Asia, and the likelihood of the disinflationary benefit the west has received in recent decades
continuing. Historically, labour cost arbitrage was one of the key drivers of declining goods prices.
While still possible, we expect the next phase of manufacturing development to instead be driven by
capital, technology and energy. Many end users and the manufacturers undertaking production are

also increasingly prioritising resilience, security and specialisation over cost minimisation. It is therefore
more probably future supply-chain developments will help contain rising costs through productivity than
drive sustained price declines for the goods bought by consumers and businesses.

Document 298: Europe is de-risking without deglobalising

European ﬁrms have not reshored production as expected.
Instead, M&A deals point to greater control over supply chains,
especially in strategic industries. Cross-border links remain, but
sustaining them requires more investment and could make
resilience costlier
Pablo Muylle
European ﬁrms are strengthening control over cross-border supply chains
rather than reshoring
Supply chain risk has moved up the policy
agenda
The pressures on European supply chains have changed since the

Covid pandemic.
Logistics disruptions and input shortages have become less
prominent, while tariﬀs, regulatory requirements and geopolitical
uncertainty increasingly shape companies’ supply-chain decisions.
Recent evidence from the European Investment Bank reinforces this
shift: reported obstacles related to raw materials, semiconductors

Share of EU ﬁrms reporting selected obstacles; 2023 and 2025 where

Reducing strategic dependencies has also become an explicit part
of the EU’s competitiveness agenda. In its 16 October conclusions,

the European Council called for renewed eﬀorts to reduce
dependencies alongside measures to promote industrial renewal,
innovation and investment.
This reﬂects a broader shift in European economic policy. Supply
chain resilience is increasingly being considered alongside
industrial renewal, innovation and competitiveness. The challenge
is to reduce critical dependencies without triggering a broader
retreat from international trade.
Despite this growing policy focus, the corporate response has not

centred on bringing production home. Previous ING research found
that reshoring accounted for less than 1% of recorded euro-area
restructuring events since 2019, compared with around 4% for
oﬀshoring.

### Page 3

But relocation is not the only way to reorganise a supply chain.
Companies can also seek greater control by acquiring suppliers or
ﬁrms operating at another stage of production. Production may
remain in the same place, and goods may continue to cross the
same borders, but the relationship changes from an arm’s-length
transaction to one in which the buyer has greater inﬂuence.
This distinction matters for the macroeconomic consequences of
de-risking. Reshoring changes the geography of production,

employment and trade. Vertical integration can leave these
patterns largely intact, while requiring ﬁrms to commit more capital
and giving them greater inﬂuence over investment, capacity and
access to inputs.
De-risking through ownership may therefore increase corporate
ﬁnancing needs without producing a comparable rise in domestic
output or employment. Its macroeconomic footprint could increase
less through the relocation of production and more through capital

allocation, ﬁnancing requirements and potentially higher
production costs. Supply chains may become more controlled and
more regional without European economies necessarily becoming
more self-suﬃcient.
To see whether ﬁrms are responding in this way, we have analysed
M&A transactions by non-ﬁnancial EU companies.
Vertical acquisitions have become more
common
Our results show a clear increase in vertically related acquisitions
since the middle of the last decade. A deal is considered vertically

related when the acquirer and target operate at diﬀerent stages of
the same supply chain.
On a 12-month moving-average basis, the share of such deals fell
to around 12% in 2015 before rising steadily. It reached almost
18% in 2023 and, after subsequently easing, stands at roughly
16% to 17% in the latest observations.

The vertical share of European acquisitions
has risen since 2015
Vertically related deals as a share of acquisitions by EU non-ﬁnancial
ﬁrms, 12-month average. A deal is considered vertically related when the
acquirer and target operate at diﬀerent stages of the same supply chain,
deﬁned as an input-output link of at least 5% between their industries.
Source: LSEG Workspace, Eurostat FIGARO input-output tables, ING
Economic Research
The upward evolution predates the pandemic, suggesting that the

shift towards greater control began before supply-chain resilience
became a prominent boardroom concern. Nevertheless, the
increase accelerated during a period in which companies faced
repeated disruptions and governments attached greater strategic
importance to supply chains.
The result should still be interpreted carefully. A vertical acquisition
does not always mean that a company is securing a critical
supplier. Some transactions, for example, reﬂect ordinary

consolidation, technological change or the pursuit of commercial
synergies. The aggregate trend, however, suggests that deals
connecting diﬀerent stages of production have become a more
important part of European M&A activity.
Deal counts provide the clearest signal. Deal values show a similar
pattern but are more volatile because many values are undisclosed,
and a few large transactions can dominate the results. We therefore
use values only as supporting evidence.

Strategic industries have a persistently
higher vertical deal share
The rise in vertical integration is not conﬁned to strategic sectors.
For this analysis, we deﬁne strategic industries as critical materials
and metals, energy, chemicals and pharmaceuticals, electronics
and electrical equipment, and machinery.
Other industries also recorded a meaningful increase, pointing to a
broader change in how European companies organise production.
Yet vertically related acquisitions remain consistently more

common in strategic industries. Their share rose from 16.6% in
2010-14 to 21.6% in 2023-25, compared with an increase from
12.0% to 15.6% in other industries.

consistently more common in strategic

pharmaceuticals, electronics, electrical equipment and critical
materials can quickly aﬀect production further down the supply
chain. These industries are also increasingly exposed to export

controls, subsidies, local-content requirements and other policy
interventions. For ﬁrms operating in this environment, ownership
can provide more control over production, investment and capacity
allocation than an ordinary supply contract.
To understand what is driving this pattern, we next focus on
upstream acquisitions, where companies acquire suppliers or other
ﬁrms at an earlier stage of production. These deals are particularly
relevant to de-risking because they can secure access to critical

inputs.
The sector-level results show that the move towards upstream
ownership is selective. The clearest increase is in critical materials
and metals, where upstream deals rose from 16.5% of acquisitions
in 2015-19 to 20.3% in 2023-25. Energy increased from 4.8% to
8.1%, while chemicals and pharmaceuticals recorded a smaller rise
from 6.1% to 6.7%. By contrast, upstream acquisitions were
broadly stable in electronics and electrical equipment and declined

in machinery.

European corporate sector and remains particularly prevalent in

Raw materials and energy can be diﬃcult to substitute at short
notice, while interruptions can halt production across several
downstream industries. The value of securing access through
ownership is therefore likely to be greatest where the consequences

of losing an input are most severe.
Upstream acquisitions are becoming more
European
Same-country targets remain dominant, accounting for 58.5% of
upstream acquisitions in 2023-2025. That is little changed from
57.8% in 2010-14. There is therefore no clear shift towards purely
domestic ownership.
The more notable development is within the remaining
transactions. The share of targets located in another EU country
increased from 18.1% to 24.8%, while the share outside the EU fell

from 24.1% to 16.7%.

This looks more like Europeanisation of ownership than reshoring.
Domestic targets remain dominant, accounting for 58.5% of
upstream acquisitions in 2023-25, and that share has barely
changed. What has changed is the composition of cross-border
acquisitions: targets elsewhere in the EU have gained share at the

expense of targets outside the bloc. The single market may
therefore oﬀer ﬁrms a way to secure greater control within a
common regulatory framework, without concentrating ownership
in their home country.
What greater corporate control means for
the European economy
The macroeconomic consequences diﬀer from those of reshoring.
Because production is not necessarily being relocated, vertical
integration may leave the geography of employment, output and

trade largely unchanged. Its eﬀects are more likely to arise through
corporate investment, ﬁnancing requirements and the economy’s
exposure to supply shocks.
Acquiring a supplier is considerably more capital-intensive than
adding another supplier or changing a contract. It can give ﬁrms
greater inﬂuence over investment, production and capacity, but
also commits capital that could otherwise ﬁnance innovation or
expansion. The implications for productivity are therefore

### Page 9

ambiguous. Closer coordination may reduce costly interruptions
and encourage supplier-speciﬁc investment, but ownership can
also make it harder to switch suppliers or technologies.
The same trade-oﬀ applies to costs. Greater control can reduce the
risk of acute shortages, but acquisitions, diversiﬁcation, additional
inventories and regional sourcing all carry a price. The emerging
model may therefore produce fewer severe disruptions without
necessarily lowering average production costs.

Ownership is one part of a broader
resilience strategy
Ownership is only one route to greater resilience. Companies can
also add suppliers, deepen contractual relationships, reserve
capacity, or hold more inventories.
Recent evidence from the European Investment Bank suggests
ﬁrms are moving away from some of the emergency responses
used during the pandemic. The share of EU importers adjusting
their supply chains fell from 50% in 2023 to 37% in 2025, while the

use of stockpiling almost halved to 17%. The EIB also ﬁnds that
ﬁrms increasingly rely on supplier and trading-partner
diversiﬁcation.
This helps place the M&A evidence in context. Vertical acquisitions
sit at the most intensive end of a broader adjustment and are likely
to be used where an input is diﬃcult to replace or where greater
inﬂuence over investment and capacity justiﬁes the ﬁnancial
commitment.
Greater resilience will not come cheaply
The emerging supply chain model places greater value on control,

visibility and redundancy. But each response comes with costs.
Acquisitions tie up capital, diversiﬁcation increases procurement
and monitoring expenses, and inventories absorb working capital.
Regional suppliers may be more reliable, but also more expensive.
These costs can be seen as an insurance premium. They may
reduce eﬃciency in normal times, but limit the damage caused by

tariﬀs, export controls, transport disruption or shortages.
For policymakers, the ﬁndings are a reminder that de-risking will

not necessarily appear as reshoring. Companies can change the
ownership and geography of their supply chains without relocating
production. Measures intended to strengthen strategic value chains
may therefore encourage cross-border European investment as well
as domestic production.
European ﬁrms are not retreating from international supply chains
so much as reorganising control within them. The result could be a
more resilient and regionally integrated production model, but not

a costless one: ﬁrms are eﬀectively paying a resilience premium in
exchange for greater control and fewer damaging disruptions.

Document 300: Eurozone_ Amid rising inflation, the business climate remains resilient, but consumer confidence falls back (2026-10-02)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Eurozone_ Amid rising inflation, the business climate remains resilient, but consumer confidence falls back (2026-10-02).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas

### Page 1

September 2026: Marked improvement in
services business confidence
September 2026: Business climate in the
manufacturing sector comfortably in the
expansion zone for the 8th month in a row
September 2026: Strengthening inflationary
pressures

to a 3-year high
Eurozone: Amid rising inflation, the business climate remains resilient, but consumer

confidence falls back
02/10/2026 3
We have selected two inflation measures (with and without energy) and six survey indicators: 1/ & 2/ business confidence, as
measured by the PMIs in the manufacturing and services sectors; 3/ & 4/ input and output prices components of the
composite PMI (in order to identify direct inflationary pressures); 5/ the suppliers’ delivery times component of the
manufacturing PMI (a direct indicator of possible supply difficulties and supply-demand imbalance, and therefore, indirectly,

of inflationary pressures in the making); and 6/ consumer confidence (measured by the “assessment of financial situation in
the next 12 months” in order to capture the impact of inflation on purchasing power). The trends in each of these indicators
are observed relative to month m=0, corresponding to the start of the conflict. Each line does not represent the level of the
indicator, but its cumulative variation compared to month m=0.
The overall picture from the confidence surveys available for September is globally positive. According to the PMIs, the

business climate in services has indeed seen a clear improvement, and that in the manufacturing sector remains comfortably
in expansion territory for the 8 th month in a row. However, consumer confidence has recorded its first decline in 5 months,
likely driven by rising inflation and concerns over purchasing power. News on the inflation front is negative. PMI surveys
report a strengthening of inflationary pressures: this trend is not a surprise given the tensions in energy prices, but it is

nonetheless significant. As for inflation itself, it continues to rise, more markedly in September: +0.6 percentage points, to
3.8% year-on-year (Eurostat flash estimate), of which 0.4 pp du to the higher energy contribution. Inflation excluding energy
increased as well, by 0.2 pp to 2.3% (of which 0.1 pp due to the rise in food inflation).
Source: S&P Global, Eurostat, European Commission, Macrobond, BNP Paribas

Source PDF: all report pdf/Ken Griffin详解“AI大跌”：算力类似航空，“边际定价”、“折旧期限”与“企业预算”三大陷阱.pdf

### Page 1

Citadel 创始⼈ Ken Griffin 认为 AI 算⼒投资有三⼤潜在陷阱： GPU 与航空公司同构，⼀旦供
过于求边际价格将⾃由落体；⽼旧 GPU 的⾼租⾦溢价难以为继，投资机构 7 ⾄ 9 年折旧模型
⾯临重估；企业端 AI ⽀出已从试错转向 ROI 精算、能省则省，与巨头天量资本⽀出形成⽭
盾。
摘要：
10 ⽉ 21 ⽇，在 Citadel 年度全球市场未来峰会上， Griffin 接受彭博记者 Dani Burger 采访时表⽰，
市场正从过去两年的 " 主题式扫货 " 转向真正意义上的基本⾯研究。他说： " 我认为这个市场正在
经历的，是⼈们真正开始做功课。 "
过去两年，市场逻辑简单粗暴：数据中⼼万亿级建设潮开启，买⼊全产业链卡位节点 —— 英伟
达、⾼带宽内存 DRAM 、超微多层陶瓷电容器 MLCC ，以及韩国、中国台湾、⽇本供应商和云计
算巨头 —— ⼏乎稳赚。如今，这套逻辑正在被重新审视。近⽇， AI 交易也遭遇⼤幅重挫，半导体
板块遭遇了⾦融危机以来最⻓连跌。

Ken Griffin 警告： GPU 与航空公司⾼度相似，边际运⾏成本极低，⼀旦算⼒供过于求，现货租赁
与 API 调⽤价格将瞬间崩塌，重演 2000 年电信泡沫中 " 暗光纤 " 的覆辙。
陷阱⼀：边际定价崩塌 ——GPU 的 “ 航空公司时刻 ”
Griffin ⽤航空业打了⼀个直接的⽐⽅。
"GPU 和航空公司⾮常相似，边际运⾏成本极低。航班上有⼀个空座，哪怕只卖 20 美元，那个座
位也是赚钱的。 " 他说， " 正是这⼀特性决定了，⼀旦航空公司出现运⼒过剩，定价就会⾃由落
体。 "
他随即将这⼀逻辑延伸⾄算⼒市场： " 如果我们到达⼀个 GPU 供给超过需求的节点，边际定价实
际上将会⾃由落体。 "
Griffin 援引 2000 年电信泡沫作为历史参照：当年⼤量铺设的光纤，因需求不⾜从未被点亮，成
为 " 暗光纤 " ，电信价格随之直线下跌。他的问题是：算⼒市场是否正⾛向同⼀条路？
" 这将彻底颠覆⼈们今天所依赖的所有投资假设。这是第⼀个问题。 "

" 现在有⼈⽤ 5 ⾄ 6 年前的⽼旧 GPU ，依然能收取极⾼的租⾦。这种情况在未来 5 ⾄ 6 年⾥⼤概率不
会持续。 " 他说， " 因此，越来越多的⼈开始担忧：我不能再像 12 个⽉前那样，对⼀块芯⽚的第

⼒。
陷阱三：企业预算收紧 ——ROI 精算时代来临

" 美国最精明的企业，正在变得⾮常清醒 —— 哪些业务需要为 OpenAI 和 Anthropic 的顶尖模型⽀
付极⾼价格，哪些业务可以⽤开源模型以极低成本运⾏。 "
他补充道： " 如果你看看那些在内部真正⽤好 AI 的公司，它们正在迅速变得⾮常善于最⼤化每⼀
分 AI ⽀出的回报。我认为整个企业界都在看着来⾃ Anthropic 和 OpenAI 的账单，然后说：好，我

们需要想清楚，怎么确保我们真的从中获得了⽣产⼒。 "
7500 亿美元与企业缩减账单的正⾯碰撞
这⼀企业端收紧趋势，与 AI ⼤模型公司的扩张计划形成了直接⽭盾。
据《华尔街⽇报》报道， Anthropic 在其 S-1 ⽂件中披露，未来计划投⼊ 7500 亿美元算⼒开⽀，
且⽀出规模仍在增加。
记者 Dani Burger 直接问 Griffin ：这两件事之间是否存在张⼒？
Griffin 的回答简短⽽直接： " 是的。你在提问的时候就已经回答了这个问题。 "
他随后指出，问题的关键在于 Anthropic 能否将⾃⾝模型打造成 " 必需品 "—— 例如在⽣命科学领
域，若其模型真正能够帮助攻克癌症或其他致命疾病，那么每家制药公司都将不惜任何代价购
买其技术。 " 但我们现在不知道答案。 "
泡沫之问：历史总是相似，但结局未必相同
对于市场上⽇益升温的 "AI 泡沫 " 讨论， Griffin 给出了⼀个有历史纵深的回答。
" 在这个时刻，⼏乎可以肯定存在局部泡沫。任何对经济的根本性变⾰，通常都与泡沫交织在⼀

起。 " 他说， " 我只希望我当年留住了亚⻢逊的股票。 "
他的判断是：泡沫破裂后留下的，往往是改变世界的技术基础设施，⽽穿越泡沫的公司，将成
为未来数⼗年的市场领导者。
但他同时强调，这并不意味着当下的定价⻛险可以被忽视 —— 尤其是在边际定价、折旧假设和
企业预算这三个维度上，市场的重新定价才刚刚开始。

### Page 3

市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

### Page 1

Asia Trends

2026 年10 月 22 日

1/3 
NZ ドル相場の行方を左右する総選挙と RBNZ の政策運営 
～インフレ圧力は根強い一方、政権交代に伴う政策枠組みの変更が不透明要因に～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ ニュージーランドでは、インフレ高進を背景に RBNZ が９月に２会合連続の利上げを実施し
たものの、11 月７日の総選挙を巡る不透明感がＮＺドル相場の重しとなってきた。 新興政党
のオポチュニティ党が連立協議のカギを握る可能性が高まっているうえ、仮に左派・労働党
を中心とする政権への交代が実現すれば、RBNZ の政策目標に雇用の最大化が再び加えられる
と見込まれる。そのため、追加利上げのハードルが高まる可能性が意識されている。 
○ ７-９月のインフレ率は前年比＋3.7％と鈍化し、市場予想も下回ったものの、引き続き目標
レンジを上回っている。エネルギーや食料品を中心にインフレ圧力が後退する一方、サービ
ス物価には上昇圧力が広がる兆しがうかがえる。さらに、原油価格の高止まりや干ばつによ
る食料インフレの懸念も残る。金融市場では早期利上げ観測がくすぶるものの、今回の物価
統計を受けて見方が修正される可能性もあり、先行きのＮＺドル相場は総選挙の結果と、そ
の後のRBNZ の政策枠組みに関する議論に左右される展開が予想される。

【RBNZは追加利上げ実施も、総選挙を巡る不透明感がNZドル相場の重しとなる展開が続いた】 
ニュージーランドでは、RBNZ（ニュージーランド準備銀行）が９月の定例理事会において２会合
連続の利上げを決定した （注１） 。 背景には、2025 年以降のインフレ率が加速してRBNZ の定める目
標レンジ（１～３％）の上限を上回る伸びで推移していることがある。さらに、イラン情勢の悪化
による原油価格の上昇がエネルギーインフレを招き、４-６月のインフレ率は前年比＋4.1％と2023
年 10-12 月以来の高い伸びとなった。こうした事情は、RBNZ の断続的な利上げ実施を後押ししたと
考えられる。 
一方、通貨ＮＺドル相場は上値の重い展開をみせてきた。同国では、11 月７日に議会（代議院：
一院制 （総議席数120） ） の総選挙の実施が予定されており、選挙結果を巡る不透明感が影響してい
るとみられる。世論調査によれば、右派の国民党、ないし左派の労働党を中心とする連立政権がい
ずれも多数派を形成できず、新興政党・オポチュニティ党がキャスティングボートを握る可能性が

注１ ９月２日付レポート「RBNZ は緩やかな引き締め示唆､NZ ドル相場の行方はどうなる？」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/654752.html）

2/3 
浮上している（注２） 。 同党は、都市部で年間1.75％、 農村部で0.5％の土地税を新設し、 それを財
源の一部に原則18 歳以上の国民に年間1.94 万ＮＺドルのベーシックインカム （ＢＩ） に相当する
「市民所得」を支給する税制改正を公約に掲げる。国民党、労働党ともに同党が掲げる税制改正案
の採用を否定しており、実現のハードルは高い。しかし、それ以外の政策の方向性については、左
派陣営と比較的親和性が高いとみられるため、選挙後に行われる連立協議の行方が注目される。 
こうしたなか、政治的な争点のひとつにRBNZ（ニュージーランド準備銀行）の政策運営が浮上し
ていることも注目される。労働党を中心
とする左派陣営 は、RBNZ のマンデート

の、追加利上げのハードルが高まることは避けられない。こうした事情も、ＮＺドル相場の重しに
なってきたと考えられる。

し（前年比＋3.9％）を下回るとともに、
事前の市場予想をも下回る伸びにとど
まった。前期比も＋0.66％と前期 （同＋
1.49％）から上昇ペースが鈍化してお
り、 原油価格の上昇一服を受けてガソリンなどエネルギー価格が下落したほか、 生鮮品をはじめと

Editorial 
Opportunities from the bond sell-off  
Strategy summary: The global bond sell-off is creating tactical 
and medium-term opportunities. In the US, September’s yield 
surge has improved the risk -reward in 10 -year government 
bonds, presenting an attractive entry point for investors with a 
6-12-month horizon. We believe bond and money markets are 
overly hawkish about the Fed outlook, given disinflation is likely 
to return as impact of oil prices and tariffs ease next year. 
In equities, we stick to quality, especially sectors riding the AI -
led earnings boom. The upcoming Q3 earnings season is likely 
to show AI -investments are powering sectors beyond the 
technology and communications services, such as financials, 
materials and power and electrification. We remain constructive 
on the semiconductor and memory segments. We also expect 
the US Dollar to peak in the next few weeks along with short -
to-medium-term US bond yields. This creates opportunities for 
AUD corporate bonds, Emerging Market USD bonds and gold. 
Attractive risk -reward in US 10 -year government bonds.  
The US 10-year bond yield rose more than 50bps in September 
(biggest monthly rise since 2022) and 115bps this year, amid 
expectations of economic resilience, higher Fed rates, fuel -
driven inflation, AI-related capital demand and fiscal concerns. 
Valuations appear close to fair value, improving the 6-12-month 
risk-reward. Bond arithmetic suggests losses of less than 1% if 
the yield rises to 6%, vs. more than 10% gains if it falls to 4.5%. 
Resilient US economic activity, but softer -than-expected 
inflation. September data showed robust US employment 
(jobless claims, ADP) and core capital-goods orders. However, 
consumer confidence remains depressed, real disposable 
income is flat and households are drawing on savings to sustain 
spending. Against this divided backdrop, core PCE inflation - 
the Fed’s preferred gauge - was softer than expected at 0.2% 
m/m and 3.0% y/y. A revised method to measure PCE inflation 
also indicate that annual core PCE inflation was 35bps lower 
than previously reported, narrowing the gap with CPI inflation. 
Markets appear overly hawkish on the Fed. Money markets 
price about 85bps of tightening over the next 12 months. A 
strong labour market (September’s non -farm payrolls data is 
due tonight) may justify one or two further hikes, but aggressive 
tightening after the sharp rise in bond yields – which has 
already tightened financial conditions - could threaten financial 
stability. US small- and mid-cap equities have already fallen 8–
9% from recent peaks over the past couple of months, 
materially underperforming large caps, as energy and 
borrowing-cost concerns intensify. W hile some Fed speakers 
remain hawkish, we  agree with New York Fed President 
Williams’ that there is no urgency to tighten. We see little 
chance of an October Fed rate hike and expect inflation data 
for Sep-Nov determining whether the Fed moves in December. 
Stay with quality equities and AI beneficiaries.  US large 
caps have absorbed the rise in bond yields relatively well, 
supported by expectations of robust earnings growth led by AI-
related investment. We continue to favour quality companies 
and sectors benefiting from this AI cycle. Semiconductors and 
memory remain preferred exposures: structural AI demand 
should extend the memory upcycle, while long -term supply 
agreements provide better revenue visibility. The opportunity is 
also broadening beyond technology and communication 
services into financials, materials, power and electrification. 
Opportunities in AUD corporate bonds and gold.  The USD 
rebound alongside higher US yields offers a tactical opportunity 
to add higher -yielding Emerging Market USD and non -USD 
bonds. We remain positive on AUD corporate bonds, which 
offer a yield premium over comparable US credit. Our 
constructive medium-term AUD/USD view could add returns for 
USD-based investors over the next 6 –12 months. We would 
also use gold’s recent pullback to add exposure, supported by 
sustained Emerging Market central bank demand. We continue 
to view USD 4,000/oz as important technical support for gold.  
—  Rajat Bhattacharya

### Page 4

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions 
  Do you expect more RBA rate hikes after its less hawkish 
guidance? What is your view on the AUD and AUD bonds? 
Our view: We expect the RBA to leave rates unchanged, but 
retain the option to hike again should inflation intensify. We 
maintain our medium-term constructive AUD view and maintain 
our opportunistically bullish AUD corporate bonds idea. 
Rationale: The RBA’s forward guidance remains data dependent, 
explicitly allowing further tightening if needed as inflation remains too 
high.  At 4.60%, Australia’s cash rate is currently 60–85bp above the 
Fed’s 3.75–4.00% range. Thus, even with our two additional 25bp s 
Fed hikes expectation, Australian rates would hold a modest positive 
rate differential. We expect the AUD to strengthen once the Fed 
signals that US rates are near their peak, which should allow resilient 
Australian fundamentals to reassert themselves. Key risks are 
persistently high US yields, weaker China growth or a sharper 
deterioration in global risk sentiment. 
We believe risk-reward has become more attractive for AUD bonds 
following the bear -flattening of the AUD yield curve. Markets are 
already pricing in one more RBA hike through end-2027, presenting 
an attractive opportunity to earn a yield even as increasingly 
restrictive financial conditions reinforc e disinflation over time. 
Meanwhile, the positive interest rate differential with the US supports 
the currency outlook, supporting the case for AUD bonds. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  What does the incoming US data tell us about the path for 
US 10-year bond yields from here? 
Our view: The risk -reward in US 10 -year yields has turned 
attractive following their move higher amid incoming US data. 
Rationale: Recent US data has indeed started to show softer-than-
expected inflation. However, market technicals are likely temporarily 
delaying a move lower in bond yields, with reports suggesting the  
rise in yields is creating forced selling amongst hedge funds and real 
estate investment trusts. Nevertheless, we do not expect this to be 
an extended selloff. We have initiated an opportunistic bullish idea 
on US 10-year government bonds this week and continue to believe 
this idea offers an attractive risk/reward on a 6-12-month horizon as 
yields become attractive enough to entice buyer flow to return. On a 
broader allocation basis, we retain our structural preference for 3-7-
year maturities in bonds. 
— Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist

We remain positive on AUD corporate bonds amid 
Australia-US interest rate differentials and positive 
outlook for the AUD

### Page 5

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  How should investors position in the GBP ahead of the UK 
budget announcement next month? 
Our view: We maintain a medium-term constructive GBP view, 
but keep near-term exposure measured ahead of the 28 October 
Budget. We would view any pre -Budget weakness as an 
opportunity to gradually build GBP exposure. 
Rationale: The government’s decision to adjust the pension triple 
lock from 2030 is an early indication of greater willingness to tackle 
longer-term spending pressures, reinforcing Chancellor Healey’s 
commitment to fiscal discipline and meeting the fiscal rules. Markets 
are likely to scrutinise the Budget closely for evidence that this 
discipline extends to near -term tax and spending decisions. A 
credible Budget should help contain pressure on UK government 
bond yields and support GBP. Any fiscal slippage remains a key risk.  
Near term, we prefer measured GBP exposure as elevated US yields 
and repricing towards further Fed tightening could continue to 
support the USD. Over the medium term, we expect GBP to rebound 
as the Fed eventually signals the peak of its tightening cycle. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  How would different US mid-term election outcome 
scenarios affect the outlook for US equities? 
Our view: We believe election outcomes will have a limited 
impact over the next 12 months. We remain positive on US 
equities driven by fundamental earnings growth.  
Rationale: Over the past 50 years, the ruling US President’s party 
has retained control of both chambers of Congress on only two 
occasions (1978 and 2002). All other elections result ed in an 
opposition sweep or a divided government.  
Based on the latest prediction markets, it appears that an opposition 
sweep (both the House and Senate flips from Republican to 
Democrats) is more likely than a divided government ( in which the 
House flips to Democrats , while the Senate remains under 
Republican control). A scenario where Republicans retain a majority 
is least likely per prediction markets. 
While mid -term elections may introduce near -term volatility, we 
caution against overreaction. Historically, the average return for the 
S&P 500 following an opposition sweep is 17% over the next 12 
months, but the average return following a divided government is not 
significantly worse, at 15%. We prefer to base our investment views 
on fundamental earnings growth, where investor attention should 
focus on the upcoming Q3 earnings season. 
—  Jason Wong, Senior Equity Analyst

opportunity to gradually build GBP exposure over

### Page 6

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 2 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What is the outlook for  the memory chip and broader 
semiconductors industry? 
Our view: We remain positive on semiconductors and memory 
chips. Structural AI demand should prolong the memory 
upcycle while long-term agreements improve revenue visibility. 
Rationale: Recent industry commentary points to tighter memory 
supply-demand conditions through 2028, reinforcing our view that AI 
demand is likely to extend the memory upcycle. Furthermore, adoption 
of long-term agreements improves revenue and earnings visibility. 
Despite debates around “Pacing the Frontier”, we have seen a flurry 
of new model releases, along with the introduction of new consumer 
AI agents capable of comparing prices online and making 
purchases. These are early days, but the path is open for a business-
to-agent (B2A) economy where businesses increasingly interact with 
AI assistants acting on customers’ behalf. As these applications are 
increasingly inference intensive, wider adoption should support 
demand for memory, with its share of global semiconductor 
revenues projected to rise from 30.7% in 2025 to 66.7% in 2027. 
Against this backdrop, we maintain our Opportunistic idea on Global 
Semiconductors. Given memory  chips’ cyclical nature, investors 
should avoid overconcentration in memory names and maintain a 
diversified exposure across Big Tech and semiconductors. 
—  Ryan Goh, Investment Strategist 
  Does a US bond yield stress test raise the risk to your 
constructive view on US equities? 
Our view: We have a target for the S&P 500 index of 8400 over 
the next 12 months. A scenario of a sustained rise in the US 10-
year government bond yield to 5.6% could lead to negative 
returns from the current S&P500 level (7,666). 
Rationale: Our base case 12-month target for the S&P 500 is 8,400, 
which assumes 2027 S&P 500 EPS of USD 420 and a 20x forward 
P/E multiple. A rise in bond yields would have an impact on equity 
valuations, as a higher discount rate reduces the present value of 
future earnings. W e stress -test our target under different 10 -year 
yield and earnings scenarios.  
A rise in bond yields would arguably be associated with a stronger 
growth environment and hence, even higher earnings. However, 
assuming unchanged EPS at our base case of USD 420, a 
(sustained) rise in the 10 -year bond yield to 5.6% would lead to 
negative returns from the current level  if not offset by a further 
improvement in earnings growth . However, in o ur base case  we 
expect the 10-year yield is more likely to fall from here. 
—  Fook Hien Yap, Senior Investment Strategist

With the ongoing major bond selloff, we asked respondents to rank what they
thought were the most important drivers. Inflation, fiscal fears, and stronger-than-
expected US growth were picked as the most important drivers. 30% picked
inflation as the most important driver even though 10yr US Breakevens have hardly

moved in the selloff.
Thinking about the recent rise in US Treasury yields, please rank the following factors in order of importance,
from the most important driver to the least important. Please drag and drop the factors into the order that
best represents your view of their importance.
Source: dbDataInsights Survey, Deutsche Bank Research

To highlight the inconsistency, US inflation expectations over the next 5 years
have risen only marginally since we last asked in June, back up to 2.9% from 2.8%.
5yr European inflation expectations are unchanged at 2.4%. So while there is a
strong narrative that inflation is causing the bond selloff, it doesn’t stack up with
long-term inflation expectations.

Despite US Treasury yields reaching multi-decade highs in September, most
respondents expect the peak in rates to be higher over the next 12 months.
The average expected peak in the 10-year yield is 5.70%, with most expecting
around 5.5% but with a longish tail and a secondary peak around 6%.
Over the next 12 months, what do you expect will be the peak level reached by the 10-year US
Treasury yield? For reference, the most recent intraday peak was 5.22%.

Document 394: Top of Mind - Assessing a Less Transparent Fed

Source PDF: all report pdf/Top of Mind - Assessing a Less Transparent Fed.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs (Top of Mind)
Date: 2026-08-25

Status: ok

### Page 1

The Goldman Sachs Group, Inc.
Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh is steering the Fed into a less transparent era. So, is that
a positive or negative development, and what could it mean for markets and the
economy? We speak with Former Fed governors Donald Kohn and Stephen Miran
as well as GS’ Jan Hatzius who agree that rigid “Odyssean” forward guidance that
commits policymakers to a pre-set path risks policy mistakes and is therefore rarely
appropriate. But they somewhat disagree about the benefits of great er transparency

around the Fed’s reaction function. Hatzius and Kohn argue that such transparency
helps markets better anticipate and price in policy changes, accelerating monetary
policy transmission. But Miran believes that the line between committing to a path
and being transparent is clearer in theory than in practice. And while GS economists
and strategists find that reduc ed transparency leads to increased market volatility,
the question is whether this just increases the market’s noise or also improves the

market’s signal. Hatzius argues largely for the former and Miran for the latter.
Jan Hatzius, Chief Economist and Head of Global Investment Research, Goldman Sachs
“Markets price what they think the Fed will do, not what they think the Fed should do. That will remain true even if the Fed
obscures its reaction function… market guesses about the Fed’s next moves will simply become worse, leading to more
volatility... and importantly, volatility that serves no constructive economic purpose.”

Donald Kohn, former Vice Chairman of the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, Robert V . Roosa
Chair in International Economics and Senior Fellow, Brookings Institution
“I sympathize with Chairman Warsh to some extent. [But] the real problem with Warsh’s approach is his reluctance to articulate
how the FOMC sees the economy evolving… explaining the Fed’s thinking about the economic outlook is essential in helping
markets act as a stabilizing force and is a core component of accountability.”

ISSUE 151 | August 19, 2026 | 1:30 PM EDT

### Page 3

El
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 3 
Top of Mind Issue 151

New Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh is steering the Fed into a less 
transparent era characterized by shorter post-meeting 
statements, less forward guidance, and a more limited role for 
projections, marking a meaningful break from the 
transparency revolution that has defined central banking over 
the last quarter century. So, what could a less transparent Fed 
mean for markets and the economy? 
For answers, we turn to former Fed governors Donald Kohn, 
now Robert V. Roosa Chair in International Economics and 
senior fellow at the Brookings Institution, and Stephen Miran, 
now senior strategist at Hudson Bay Capital Management, as 
well as Jan Hatzius, GS’ Chief Economist and Head of Global 
Investment Research. 
We first ask whether scaling back Fed communication is a 
positive or negative development. Hatzius says it’s likely 
negative, though he draws a clear line between transparency 
and forward guidance. He argues that clarity about the Fed’s 
reaction function helps markets better anticipate policy 
changes and quickly incorporate new information into financial 
conditions, thereby accelerating monetary policy transmission 
to the economy. So, he believes that unwinding that 
transparency would be a mistake. (Note: GS Global 
Economists Joseph Briggs and Megan Peters lay out the 
evidence backing this argument on pgs. 14-15.) 
But Hatzius takes a more nuanced view of forward guidance, 
arguing that rigid “Odyssean” guidance that commits 
policymakers to a pre-set path regardless of new information 
only makes sense at the effective lower bound for the policy 
rate, while conditional “Delphic” guidance tied to the 
economic outlook is generally more defensible. Although 
markets could confuse the two, he believes that most 
sophisticated investors understand the difference and is 
therefore not particularly worried that markets have become 
overly reliant on the policy dots published in the Fed’s 
Summary of Economic Projections (SEP). Instead, he 
considers them a “useful source of additional information 
about the Fed’s reaction function” (except for the long-run 
policy dot). If anything, Hatzius would prefer the dots move 
in an even more informative direction—by publishing each 
policymaker’s combined projections for growth, inflation, 
unemployment, and the policy rate on an anonymized basis. 
Kohn agrees that clarity about the Fed’s “narrative” and 
reaction function is vital. Explaining how the Fed sees the 
economy evolving and how policy would respond, he says, is 
“essential in helping markets act as a stabilizing force and is 
a core component of accountability.” Still, he sympathizes 
somewhat with Chairman Warsh’s view that the dot plot is 
“not helpful” (see pg. 9 for Warsh’s own words on this), 
arguing that the market’s hyper-focus on the SEP’s median 
policy dot “illustrates the risk of excessive precision and 
false certainty,” as small shifts in it can trigger unwarranted 
market moves. So, he has long favored omitting the median. 
Miran, though, is more supportive of Warsh’s push for a less 
transparent Fed. Like Hatzius and Kohn, he’s concerned that 
excessive forward guidance that locks the Fed into a policy 
path raises the risk of policy errors and also argues that such 
guidance can impair market pricing of risk. But he’s less 
convinced about the benefits of increased transparency 
around the Fed’s reaction function, making the case that the 
distinction between the Fed committing to a pre-set policy 
path and being transparent about its reaction function is clear 
in theory, but less so in practice. So, he thinks “the policy dots 
absolutely have to go” but feels less strongly about omitting 
the SEP’s economic projections. 
But would less transparency allow markets to provide the Fed 
with more “direct and unfiltered” information about the 
economy, as Warsh has argued? The answer seems to boil 
down to the implications for market volatility, and whether any 
resulting volatility would simply increase the market’s noise or 
also improve its signal. 
As for the volatility implications, the evidence is fairly clear. 
Briggs and Peters find that increased central bank 
transparency has led to a reduction in market volatility over the 
last several decades. This is consistent with GS Head of US 
Rates Strategy William Marshall’s recent observation of the 
reverse: that the US rates market has already priced 
substantial uncertainty associated with a less transparent Fed, 
with the July FOMC representing the largest non-cut meeting 
day surprise in almost three decades.  
But Marshall argues that whether such meeting-day surprises 
lead to structurally higher rates volatility depends (again) on 
whether the Fed’s reaction function is well understood. If it’s 
not, he would expect higher rates volatility to persist. GS FX 
Strategists Michael Cahill and Lexi Kanter find a similar 
pattern in FX: that central bank communication shifts can 
change which events and data drive FX volatility without 
necessarily raising it, but less transparency could increase 
Dollar volatility—all else equal (though global macro 
conditions remain its biggest driver).  
But the key debate seems to be whether the increased 
volatility resulting from less Fed transparency will improve or 
worsen the market’s signal. Hatzius is clearly in the “worsen” 
camp. He argues that markets price what they think the Fed 
will do—not what it should do—and less transparency won’t 
change that; it would simply produce worse guesses. So, he 
says that while volatility in and of itself is not necessarily bad, 
volatility driven by uncertainty about the Fed’s reaction 
function would serve no constructive economic purpose. 
Kohn agrees that the lack of a clear Fed narrative could create 
damaging uncertainty. But he argues that removing overly 
specific guidance could help free markets to process new 
information more independently and isn’t too worried about 
modestly higher volatility as a result. And Miran seems most 
convinced that reducing transparency would improve the 
markets’ signal, with volatility an unavoidable but acceptable 
cost: “The first best world of signal without volatility doesn't 
exist. I'd rather have that signal, even if it's messy, than 
throw the signal out by anchoring market expectations to the 
Fed's near-term projections.” 
Allison Nathan, Editor 
Email: allison.nathan@gs.com   
Tel: 212-357-7504   
Goldman Sachs & Co. LLC    
Assessing less Fed transparency

### Page 4

El
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 4 
Top of Mind Issue 151

Jan Hatzius is Chief Economist and Head of Global Investment Research at Goldman Sachs. 
Below, he argues that a shift toward a less transparent Fed is likely a negative development.  
Ashley Rhodes: Fed Chairman Kevin 
Warsh appears to be ushering the 
Fed into a less transparent era. Is 
that shift a positive or negative 
development? 
Jan Hatzius: It’s likely a negative 
development, although I would 
distinguish between transparency and 
forward guidance. The transparency 
revolution in central banking over the last 30 years has been 
extremely successful. When a central bank provides 
meaningful information about its reaction function—how it’s 
likely to respond to different economic outcomes—markets can 
better anticipate policy changes. So, new information gets 
incorporated quickly into financial conditions, accelerating the 
transmission of monetary policy. The central bank can later 
decide whether to ratify that move and always retains the 
option not to. That framework has worked well for the Fed over 
the past several decades. Unwinding transparency about the 
reaction function risks delaying the transmission of monetary 
policy and would be a mistake, in my view. 
Forward guidance about the policy rate path is a separate and 
more nuanced issue. In assessing the pros and cons, it’s 
important to distinguish between “Odyssean” and “Delphic” 
forward guidance. With Odyssean guidance, like Odysseus 
tying himself to the mast, the central bank commits to a policy 
path even if subsequent data point in a different direction. That 
only makes sense at the effective lower bound for the policy 
rate, when conventional monetary easing is no longer an 
option. Even then, the hurdle for Odyssean guidance should be 
high because there is a real risk that policymakers lock 
themselves into an inappropriate policy stance. By contrast, 
Delphic guidance, named for the Oracle of Delphi, is a 
conditional description of what policymakers expect to do given 
their economic outlook that will change with new information. 
Delphic guidance is more defensible, even in normal times. 
Ashley Rhodes: Let’s take these in turn. Do you agree with 
the argument that Odyssean forward guidance increases 
the risk of policy errors? 
Jan Hatzius: That argument is a serious one. In terms of 
practical examples, it is heavily centered on the 2021–2022 
episode, when the Fed tightened policy much too late because 
they made a major forecasting error on inflation at a time of 
rigid Odyssean forward guidance. That was a bad combination. 
If the forecast is wrong, policy will be set incorrectly under any 
communication or transparency framework. And Odyssean 
forward guidance compounds that problem because it delays 
the inevitable course correction. This is why Odyssean forward 
guidance should be used sparingly, if at all. 
That said, I am not sure how big a difference to the inflation 
outcome or to economic performance more broadly it would 
have made if the Fed had started to tighten in, say, September 
2021 rather than March 2022. By early 2022, markets had 
already recognized that the funds rate would need to rise, and 
financial conditions tightened substantially on the back of 
higher rate expectations well before the Fed delivered those 
hikes. Also, the error was ultimately not disastrous 
economically; inflation expectations did not de-anchor and the 
Fed course-corrected without causing a recession. 
Ashley Rhodes: Even if the Fed just provides Delphic 
guidance, are concerns that the market might confuse it 
with Odyssean guidance warranted, especially given how 
much weight markets put on the dot plot? 
Jan Hatzius: Such confusion would be problematic, but I think 
it doesn’t happen frequently in practice. Most sophisticated 
observers understand that the policy dots included in the 
Summary of Economic Projections (SEP) are conditional on data 
evolving in line with the economic projections. And the dots 
only provide information about the individual and anonymized 
views of FOMC participants, including non-voters. So, they 
aren’t strong forward guidance, not even strong Delphic 
forward guidance. And market expectations have often 
diverged meaningfully from them, undermining the argument 
that investors view them as a commitment.  
Instead, the dots are a useful source of additional information 
about the Fed’s reaction function. The SEP could be made even 
more informative by publishing each policymaker’s combined 
projections for growth, inflation, unemployment, and the Fed 
funds rate on an anonymous basis, which we have long argued 
for. Doing so would provide a clearer picture of how 
policymakers’ economic forecasts relate to their anticipated 
policy paths. But that seems unlikely given Warsh’s apparent 
preference for moving in the opposite direction. 
Concerns about market confusion are more understandable 
when it comes to the long-run policy dot, where the Fed has 
less information to convey. Nobody knows where R-star is—as 
Chair Powell liked to say, “it is seen by its works”—but 
markets seem to assume that the Fed has special knowledge 
and therefore place too much weight on the long-term dot. 
That can lock down longer-term rate expectations too firmly. 
That said, removing the long-run dot could complicate the Fed’s 
communication in its current form, as it has become an 
important benchmark for assessing the stance of monetary 
policy. I would prefer to eliminate the long-run dot and 
communicate the stance of policy with greater emphasis on 
financial conditions. Overall, though, the dot plot works well, 
and, despite the controversy surrounding it, I remain a fan. 
Ashley Rhodes: Aren’t the Fed’s inflation and employment 
targets sufficient for the market to infer the Fed’s reaction? 
Jan Hatzius: No, because getting from point A to point B 
requires more information than just a stated objective. Take the 
inflation target, for example. The Fed's goal is 2% inflation, but 
how do you assess whether inflation is moving sustainably 
toward that target? How do you determine the current 
underlying rate of inflation? Should you look at headline PCE, 
core PCE, trimmed mean inflation, or something else entirely? 
Do you project future inflation changes based on the 
Interview with Jan Hatzius

### Page 5

El
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 5
Top of Mind Issue 151
unemployment rate via a Phillips curve relationship, the
monetary aggregates, or something else? To anticipate the
Fed’s moves—and thereby push financial conditions in a
stabilizing direction—markets need insight into how the Fed
assesses these questions. Those considerations go well
beyond stating a broad policy goal.
Ashley Rhodes: Is there any merit to communicating less
given that Fed officials have, at times, inadvertently made

market-moving remarks?
Jan Hatzius: There is certainly an optimal level and frequency
of communication. The Fed should not provide running
commentary on every data release, especially as markets tend
to overemphasize individual data points relative to the longer-
term trend. The Reserve Bank presidents speak a lot more
frequently than the governors and their comments are almost
by definition less informative about the policy path than those
of the Fed leadership. While that can complicate the public's

understanding of the policy outlook, it can also be informative
for market participants who nevertheless understand that the
views of the troika—the Fed chairman, Fed vice chair, and New
York Fed president—carry the greatest weight. On net, it’s
therefore unclear whether noise from frequent policymaker
comments has had big practical costs. More broadly,
communication mistakes are inevitable, and policymakers will
say things they later wish they hadn't. But the question is

whether these occasional mistakes outweigh the benefits from
transparent communication. In my view, they don’t.
Ashley Rhodes: Warsh believes that providing less
guidance about the Fed’s reaction function will allow
policymakers to draw more “direct and unfiltered”
information from markets. So, you don’t agree?
Jan Hatzius: I don’t. Markets price what they think the Fed will
do, not what they think the Fed should do. That will remain true
even if the Fed obscures its reaction function. So, if the Fed

chooses to do so, market guesses about the Fed’s next moves
will simply become worse, leading to more volatility in rates
markets and in financial conditions—and importantly, volatility
that serves no constructive economic purpose. Volatility is not
bad in and of itself. For example, if new data shows increased
inflation risks, financial conditions should tighten as markets
price in rate hikes. This accelerates the transmission of
monetary policy. But volatility driven by uncertainty about the

Fed’s reaction function is more random and doesn’t necessarily
push financial conditions in a stabilizing direction.
Also, policymakers can already extract valuable information
from market indicators. For example, a sharp rise in breakeven
inflation above target is a statement that markets view the
expected policy path as inappropriately loose. So, the
information is already available without the Fed saying less.
Ashley Rhodes: Is there a risk of a “hall of mirrors” effect if

the Fed uses market prices to not only glean information
about the economy but also to guide what it should do?
Jan Hatzius: Yes, but I am not particularly concerned about
this risk. While a “hall of mirrors” effect—whereby markets
watch the Fed for clues, but the Fed watches the markets for
clues—could push the policy stance far away from desirable
levels, the likelihood of that scenario materializing is low.
Warsh has said that he does not want to “be constrained or

take verbatim from what the market’s doing.” And Fed
officials, including the Chairman, are unlikely to take that logic
to its extreme conclusion and follow the market simply
because it has priced in a particular policy move.
Ashley Rhodes: Warsh has also said that financial markets
“are probably the most important source of information to
guide central bankers.” Do you agree?
Jan Hatzius: Not entirely, as I would rank the economic data
first. That said, financial market prices are very important, and

central banks have become increasingly sophisticated in
extracting information from them. There’s always room for
improvement, but again, the information is already available
under the current regime. And communicating less
transparently wouldn’t help policymakers evaluate financial
market signals more appropriately.
Ashley Rhodes: How durable is a less transparent Fed?
Jan Hatzius: That remains an open question. As Chairman,
Warsh has significant control over the Fed’s communications

framework and can choose to provide less information at press
conferences, as he has done. In that sense, a less transparent
approach could prove durable, though its consequences remain
to be seen. Other communication changes, such as eliminating
the dot plot, would probably in practice require FOMC support,
and my sense is that other Fed officials would see that as too
great a step away from transparency.
A large reduction in Fed communication, defined by the

number of speeches, is less likely to endure. Reserve Bank
presidents have strong incentives to continue talking, in part
because the influence and relevance of their institutions
depend on it. This has been a perennial issue—Chairman Alan
Greenspan was often very frustrated with the Bank presidents.
Warsh could try to rein them in. But even Greenspan in his
heyday failed to do so. If regional bank presidents continue
speaking frequently while centralized communication declines,

the cacophony could actually worsen.
Ashley Rhodes: More broadly, while the Fed held rates
steady in July, three FOMC participants dissented in favor
of a hike, fueling debate about whether further tightening
is on the horizon. Where do you stand?
Jan Hatzius: We don’t expect the Fed to hike, largely because
we think much of the recent strength in core PCE inflation
reflects temporary factors: higher oil prices related to the US-
Iran war, roughly 70bp of lingering tariff pass-through, and

distortions in software and accessories prices. As these effects
fade, we expect core inflation to move close to 2% in 2027.
That assessment is consistent with the ongoing deceleration in
other measures of underlying inflation such as trimmed-mean
PCE and core CPI.
The recent data are also broadly consistent with that view. The
inflation data improved significantly in June and July, and the
labor market reports for both months were somewhat weaker

on balance. If those trends continue, policymakers will likely
conclude that additional tightening is unnecessary, especially
with the funds rate already around 3.5-3.75%—a level the Fed
likely views as roughly neutral or even slightly restrictive.
